### S1Q02F · followup · 30 words · clip 10.6 s · first detection +1.6 s from the end of the clip · coverage 90 %

> What can you conclude from a PR AUC of 0.38 and an ROC AUC of 0.88, and what else would you need before deciding the model is useful?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.6 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Correctly pegs PR-AUC 0.38 at four times the 9.5 percent baseline and names the missing economics._

heard: "What can you conclude from a PRAUC of 0.38 and an ROC AUC of 0.88? And what else"

> An ROC-AUC of 0.88 shows strong overall ranking, but with a 9.5% churn base rate, it can be inflated. The PR-AUC of 0.38 is the key metric here—it's roughly four times the random baseline, proving it isolates high-risk users effectively under class imbalance. To confirm business utility, I need the economics: the cost of a false positive, the cost of a missed churner, and the conversion rate of our retention campaigns to verify positive ROI.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 3.7 s, 66 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Correctly explains ROC inflation under imbalance, benchmarks PR AUC against prevalence, and asks for cost-benefit evidence._

### S1Q10F · followup · 23 words · clip 10.0 s · first detection +2.0 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> The application receives an authorization error after deployment. How would you distinguish an identity problem from a permissions problem or a network problem?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +2.0 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: The 401 versus 403 versus timeout triage is exactly the right discriminator; 99 words runs long._

heard: "The application receives an authorization error after deployment. How would you "

> I'd isolate the root cause by checking the exact error response and transport behavior. If the service fails to fetch a token from Microsoft Entra ID or returns a 401, it's an identity problem tied to a misconfigured Managed Identity. If it returns a 403 or a database permission denial, the identity is valid, but it's a permissions problem requiring an update to the Azure RBAC role assignment or PostgreSQL grants. If the connection hangs or times out before hitting the auth layer, it's a network problem caused by a blocked Private Endpoint or restrictive Network Security Group rule.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 4.2 s, 140 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Sound triage using status codes, sign-in logs and in-pod connectivity checks, but about 140 words._

> I'd start by inspecting the specific HTTP status code and the response body. A 403 Forbidden usually points to an RBAC issue, while a connection timeout or "connection refused" confirms a network blockage.
> 
> To isolate the cause, I'd first check the Entra ID sign-in logs to see if the Managed Identity is even attempting to authenticate. If the logs show a successful sign-in but the request fails, it's a permissions issue—I'd audit the IAM role assignments on the PostgreSQL or Blob storage to ensure the identity has the specific "Data Reader" or "Contributor" role. If the logs show no activity, I'd verify the identity is correctly assigned to the workload resource. Finally, for network issues, I'd use tcpping or curl from within the pod to test connectivity to the private endpoint, ensuring the Network Security Group isn't dropping the traffic.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r3** — TTFT 6.0 s, 71 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)

Finally, if the request times out, it’s a network issue; I’d check the Private Endpoint connectivity and verify that DNS is resolving to the private IP.

Finally, if the request times out or returns a connection error, that’s a network issue. > An agent justifies its complexity when the execution path is non-deterministic—meaning the outcome of the first step dictates what you need to do next. For a question requiring evidence across three different documents, a fixed retrieval workflow typically fails because a single hardcoded search query misses the contextual pivots needed for the subsequent files. Query decomposition improves on this by splitting the prompt into sequential sub-queries, but it's still rigid. A dynamic agent justifies its overhead here because it can inspect the chunks returned from the first document, evaluate what's missing, and conditionally route to different tools or vector spaces to fetch the remaining evidence.

heard: "Why might you use Rank Fusion instead of adding the raw scores, and how would yo"

> I use rank fusion instead of raw scores because different retrievers like BM25 and vector search produce incomparable score scales, meaning direct addition would let whichever model has larger raw values dominate. Rank fusion avoids this by operating purely on item positions. To add configurable weights, I multiply each retriever's contribution by a source-specific weight coefficient before combining them, allowing us to easily tune the balance between keyword and semantic retrieval.

To add weights, I’d use Weighted Reciprocal Rank Fusion, multiplying each source's reciprocal rank by a configurable weight factor before summing.

### S2Q05F · followup · 22 words · clip 8.9 s · first detection −2.5 s from the end of the clip · coverage 93 %

> Construct an example where that greedy policy gives poor coverage, and tell me how you would incorporate redundancy or multi-hop dependencies.

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +4.0 s → **weak** (correctness 1 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Strong redundancy example and MMR fix, but multi-hop dependencies are never addressed; 103 words._

heard: "Construct an example where that greedy policy gives poor coverage, and tell me h"

> A greedy policy gives poor coverage when the top retrieved chunks are semantically redundant. For instance, if a user asks about both a contract's penalty clause and its termination exceptions, a greedy approach might pick the top three chunks that all detail the penalty clause because they share high keyword overlap, completely missing the termination exception chunk ranked slightly lower. To fix this, I incorporate novelty into the selection criteria using Maximal Marginal Relevance, penalizing chunks with high similarity to already selected ones. The key dependencies are the penalty hyperparameter balancing relevance against diversity, and the token budget constraints of the downstream generator.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 3.9 s, 79 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Redundant top chunks as the failure example and maximal marginal relevance as the fix, with a fair caveat._

> A greedy policy fails when the top-ranked chunks are highly redundant, like multiple paragraphs describing the same policy detail, effectively crowding out broader context. I’d switch to Maximal Marginal Relevance, or MMR, which penalizes chunks that are too similar to those already selected, forcing the system to prioritize novel information.
> 
> The critical dependency here is the embedding space's ability to capture semantic distance; if the embeddings aren't well-clustered, the diversity penalty might accidentally discard highly relevant but nuanced information.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r3** — TTFT 8.0 s, 164 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Concrete failure example plus MMR and agentic decomposition; roughly 180 words is far over spoken length._

> A greedy policy often fails on multi-hop questions where the answer is split across disparate documents. For example, if a user asks about a specific policy change that is only mentioned in a footnote of a secondary document, a relevance-first approach will likely flood the context window with the primary document's content, effectively "drowning out" the critical, lower-scoring evidence needed to synthesize the answer.
> 
> To fix this, I move away from pure relevance-first selection and incorporate a Maximum Marginal Relevance or MMR-based reranking. This forces the system to penalize chunks that are too similar to those already selected, which naturally pushes the model to include diverse, novel information rather than just redundant top-scoring hits. For multi-hop dependencies, I implement an agentic decomposition step first: the agent breaks the complex query into sub-questions, retrieves context for each independently, and then merges those specific, high-precision results into the final context window. This ensures we aren't just relying on a single retrieval pass to capture every dependency.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low** — TTFT 5.0 s, 118 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)

> A greedy relevance-first policy fails on multi-faceted queries. For example, if a user asks for the pros and cons of a system, and the top-k chunks are all highly relevant to the pros, the greedy approach will saturate the context window with redundant information while ignoring the cons.
>

> A greedy relevance-first policy fails when the top-ranked chunks are redundant, essentially repeating the same information while ignoring other parts of the query. > 
> To fix this, I incorporate Maximal Marginal Relevance, or MMR, into the selection criteria. Instead of just picking the highest score, I penalize chunks that are too similar to those already selected, which forces the system to pull in novel information. For multi-hop dependencies, I use agentic query decomposition. I break the original question into sub-questions and perform retrieval for each, ensuring that the context gathered for the second hop is informed by the findings of the first. This approach ensures the final answer is both comprehensive and logically connected.

> 
> Backfill: Run a batch job to re-embed all existing documents with the new model, writing to a separate index or collection. Use a queue to track progress and handle failures.

> Sync: For new or updated documents, dual-write to both the old and new embedding stores. A background reconciler ensures consistency if writes fail.

> 
> Cutover: Once backfill is complete and quality is validated, switch read traffic to the new model. Keep the old model warm for a rollback window.

> Rollback: If issues arise, flip read traffic back to the old model. The old embeddings remain intact, so service resumes immediately.

> For poison documents, Azure Service Bus routes failed messages to a dead-letter queue after max delivery attempts for isolated debugging before any manual replay. To handle delete events racing older updates, I use a monotonic version timestamp in Cosmos DB, ensuring late-arriving updates are automatically dropped if a delete has already been committed.

> Service Bus automatically routes poison messages exceeding max delivery counts to the dead-letter queue for offline inspection and safe replay. To handle a delete racing with an older update, I use optimistic concurrency with ETags in Cosmos DB, ensuring an incoming update checks the item version and gets rejected if a newer timestamp or delete tombstone already exists.

**Arm gemini-3.5-flash-lite_captured-high** — TTFT 3.8 s, 74 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Max delivery count to dead-letter plus version or tombstone comparison handles the delete-versus-stale-update race._

> For poison documents, I set a max delivery count on Service Bus so failing messages automatically land in the dead-letter queue for offline debugging and replay. To handle delete events racing with older updates, I use optimistic concurrency with a document version timestamp or sequence number. When processing, if the record's existing version is newer than the incoming payload—or if a delete tombstone exists—the operation is ignored, preventing stale updates from resurrecting deleted data.
